Angry that it happened to me, angry that I'm dealing with all this now, and angry that I can't just shake it off and go back to normal. But responsible? No. I was just the guy holding the box.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? I... I don't know if I'd say constantly on the lookout, exactly. I mean, I'm definitely jumpy, like I was saying before with the driving, how every little noise makes me flinch. But it's not like I'm walking around scanning rooftops for snipers or checking behind every door before I open it, you know? It's more of a... a reaction thing, rather than me actively searching for danger. \n\nLike, if I'm just sitting in my apartment, I'm not peeking through the blinds or anything. I'm just trying to survive the day. But if the floorboards creak, or if the radiator hisses too loud, I definitely jump. So, I don't think I'm constantly on the lookout, like some kind of bodyguard or something. I'm just really, really sensitive to everything happening around me right now. But actively looking for threats? No, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. I'm too exhausted to be a watchman, honestly. I just want to be left alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startled or frightened by things that wouldn't normally startle you, like a door slamming or a car driving by?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, absolutely. I think I kind of touched on that a second ago with the driving, but it's... it's honestly out of control. It's not just a little flinch, it's like my entire body goes into shock over the smallest things. Like, I was in the van the other day—well, the last day I actually tried to work—and the dispatch radio cracked. Not loud, just the normal static before someone talks. And I swear to you, I practically hit my head on the roof of the cab. I jumped so hard, my hands slipped off the steering wheel. It was like I thought someone was about to rush me right there in the driver's seat. \n\nAnd it's sirens, too. I live in the city, there are sirens all the time, right? Normally, I don't even hear them, they're just background noise. But now? A police car or an ambulance wails three blocks away, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so violently it actually hurts. It just pounds and pounds, and it doesn't stop even after the siren fades. I literally had to pull the van over to the curb twice on Tuesday because my chest was heaving and I couldn't catch my breath just from hearing a fire truck. \n\nEven inside my own building... my neighbor across the hall, he's kind of a heavy-handed guy, always lets his door slam shut when he leaves for work. He's done it for the three years I've lived there. Yesterday morning, he slammed it, and I dropped my coffee mug right on the kitchen floor. It shattered everywhere, and I just stood there shaking, like, physically vibrating, waiting for someone to bust through my door. I had to go step outside onto the fire escape for, like, twenty minutes just to get my heart rate down enough to clean up the mess. It's exhausting. Every sudden noise feels like a physical blow, like an alarm going off in my brain telling me I'm about to be attacked, and I just can't turn the alarm off.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble maintaining relationships with your friends or family due to what's been happening?\n\nPatient: Friends and family? I mean, yeah, but honestly, it’s just... it's everyone. It's not just the people I'm close to, it's literally anyone. I just can't keep it together around people right now, period. Like, I usually do this volunteer thing on the weekends—I've been doing it for a couple of years, just helping out at the community center, setting up chairs and organizing the pantry, that kind of thing—and I just completely stopped showing up for my volunteer commitments. I didn't even call them to explain, because what am I supposed to say? \"Sorry, I can't handle being in a room with other human beings without feeling like I'm going to lose my mind?\" So I just ghosted them, which makes me feel terrible because they rely on me, but I physically can't do it. I can't put on a brave face. \n\nAnd it's bleeding into everything else, too, not just my social life. My whole life is just... it's on pause. Like work. I don't feel safe driving that massive van through city traffic, and I definitely don't feel safe walking up to random buildings, going into elevators, and handling deliveries. I'm just too shaken up. I'm so completely on edge that my hands are sweating on the wheel, and I just know I'm going to cause an accident or freak out on a receptionist or something. So yeah, it's affecting my relationships, but it's also just ruining my ability to function at all. I can't work, I can't volunteer, I can't even be around people. I'm just stuck in my apartment.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy things you used to like doing before this incident happened?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? I mean... I feel like if I get behind the wheel, I'm going to crash because my hands are shaking so bad, or I'm going to have a panic attack in the middle of an intersection. And handling the deliveries? Walking into a building where I don't know who's in there, or getting into an elevator with strangers? Forget it. I'm just too shaken up, too completely on edge. I feel like a raw nerve.\n\nAnd it's not just work, it's the stuff I actually choose to do, too. My whole weekend routine is gone. I mentioned the volunteer thing... I've been helping out at this community center for a couple of years now. Every Saturday morning, I'd go in, set up chairs for the meetings, organize the food pantry, maybe help unload the delivery truck if it came early. It was a good routine, you know? It got me out of the house, I liked the people there. But I just stopped showing up. Completely ghosted them. And I feel awful about it, I really do, because they count on me to be there to get things set up before the doors open. But I just... I can't keep it together around people right now. The thought of walking into that community center, where it's loud and crowded and there are people everywhere wanting to talk or asking me to move boxes... I just can't handle it. I can't put on a normal face and pretend I'm fine when inside I feel like I'm vibrating out of my skin. So my daily tasks, my routine, my job, my volunteering... it's all just gone. I'm not doing any of it. I'm just stuck here, trying to figure out how to get through the next hour.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like you're able to function normally despite what's been happening?\n\nPatient: Function normally? Honestly, doc, no. Not at all. I mean, I know I literally just went on a whole rant about this a second ago, but... it really is just a complete, across-the-board failure to function right now. There is no \"normal\" area left. \n\nLike I was just saying about work, missing those two shifts wasn't just me needing a mental health day or something. I physically and mentally cannot do it. When I try to work, I just... I don't feel safe driving that van anymore. I don't feel safe navigating the streets or walking up to buildings and handling the deliveries, because I'm just way too shaken. I'm so on edge that the smallest thing makes me want to jump out of my skin, so how am I supposed to do a fast-paced messenger job like that? \n\nAnd it's the exact same reason I completely stopped showing up for my volunteer commitments, which I know I just mentioned too, but it really just highlights how deep this goes. I just can't keep it together around people right now. Not at work, not at the community center, not anywhere. So, no, there isn't a single part of my life that feels intact right now. Everything is just completely derailed. I don't have anywhere I can go or anything I can do where I feel like I'm actually capable of being a functioning person. It's all just broken.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always being on the lookout for potential threats or dangers, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? I... I don't know if I'd say I'm always looking for threats, exactly. It's not like I'm pacing around my apartment peeking through the blinds every five minutes or expecting someone to kick the door down, you know? I mean, I'm definitely jumpy, like I said before. I'm on edge, my nerves are shot, and I feel like I'm vibrating out of my skin half the time. But it's not really this conscious thing where I'm scanning the room for danger or constantly thinking something bad is about to happen right this second. \n\nIt's more like... I just feel unsafe in general, especially if I think about going outside or getting back in the van, but I'm not actively looking for a threat. If anything, I'm just trying to hide from everything. I'm just overwhelmed by the feeling that everything is too much, rather than looking for a specific reason to be scared, if that makes sense? I'm just trying to avoid the world entirely right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific memories or reminders of the holding room incident that you try to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there definitely are. Like, I told you I'm a courier, right? And usually, I'd just take whatever route is fastest, whatever dispatch gives me, but lately... I just can't. \n\nI've been flat-out turning down any night runs. I used to like them, actually, because the traffic is lighter and you can just cruise, but now? Outside of my body? Like... floating above myself or something? No, I... I don't think I've ever felt anything like that. When the flashes happen, when I get those memories of the holding room, it's not like a dream at all. It's actually the exact opposite of a dream. It's too real. It's so real that it feels like it's happening right then and there. 

Like I was saying before, about how the air feels the same and my stomach drops? It's not like I'm watching a movie of myself going through it from a distance. I'm *in* it. I'm right back in my own skin, feeling the same panic, the same cold sweat, the same tightness in my chest that I felt when I was actually in that room. It's completely overwhelming, but I'm definitely still inside my own body. If anything, I wish I could feel like I was outside of it, you know? Maybe then it wouldn't be so terrifying. 

But no, it's just... it's a very grounded, very physical feeling of terror. There's no detachment. I don't feel disconnected from myself, I just feel disconnected from where I actually am in the present moment, because my brain is dragging me back to that place. So, no, I haven't had any out-of-body experiences or anything like a dream state. It's just a nightmare that feels entirely real, and I'm wide awake for it.

I mean, it's... it's affecting how I do my job, which is a huge problem because I need this job, you know? I'm a courier, so I'm supposed to be flexible, but lately... man, I just can't. I've been flat-out turning down any night runs. My dispatcher is getting annoyed with me, but I just make up excuses because I can't explain to him that the idea of being out at night, especially downtown, just makes my chest tight. And speaking of downtown, I've been taking these ridiculous detours. I mean, I'll literally drive twenty minutes out of my way, taking the long way around the entire city, burning extra gas, just to make absolutely sure I don't go anywhere near that detention building. \n\nJust the thought of driving past it... I can't do it. I can't even look in that direction. And it's not just the place, either. It's... it's talking about it. Like, even right now, sitting here with you, it's hard. When my friends or my roommate ask me where I was that night, or why I look like I haven't slept in a week, I just change the subject or walk out of the room. I can't make myself talk about what happened, because the second I start trying to explain it, or even just think about the words to describe it, it's like a switch flips. My heart starts hammering, and I feel like I'm right back on that freezing metal bench. I can't even get the words out without feeling like I'm trapped back in that room, so I just avoid the whole thing. I avoid the building, I avoid the night shifts, I avoid the conversations... I'm just trying to avoid anything that reminds me of it, but it's like my whole life is suddenly a minefield.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh man, sleep is... it's basically a joke at this point. I mean, I try, you know? I go through the motions. I get home, I'm completely exhausted from trying to keep it together all day, dodging downtown, dealing with the dispatcher... I just want to crash. I'll lay down, and maybe, if I'm lucky, I'll doze off for an hour, maybe two if I'm really exhausted. But then... bam. I'm wide awake. \n\nIt's usually a dream, or like... a nightmare, I guess. I'll be right back in that cell, or I'll hear this loud noise, like the heavy metal door slamming shut, echoing down that concrete hallway, and my eyes just snap open. My heart is pounding out of my chest, I'm sweating, and I'm just staring at the ceiling in my bedroom, trying to convince myself I'm actually home and not back in that holding area. And once I'm up like that, forget it. I can't go back to sleep. I just lie there, tossing and turning, my brain just spinning and spinning, replaying the whole thing or just dreading the next day. \n\nBy the time morning rolls around and my alarm goes off, I feel completely wrecked. I mean, physically and mentally destroyed. And then I have to drag myself out of bed, get in my van, and try to drive my route, try to be a functional courier, when I'm running on maybe an hour of terrible, interrupted sleep. It's dangerous, honestly, being behind the wheel when I'm this tired and jittery. I just... I barely sleep now. It's ruining me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble interacting with friends, family, or others since this happened?\n\nPatient: Interacting with people? Yeah, it’s... honestly, it’s a disaster. I just can't do it right now. Like, usually, I have these volunteer commitments, right? I help out at this local community center on my days off, sorting donations and stuff, and I usually really like the people there, we always joke around. But since this whole thing happened, I just completely stopped showing up. I haven't gone at all. I can’t keep it together around people right now, you know? The idea of standing there, making small talk, pretending I'm fine when my chest is tight and I feel like I'm vibrating out of my own skin... I just know I'd either snap at someone for no reason or completely break down. So I just hide in my room. \n\nAnd it’s not just the volunteering, it’s bleeding into the one thing I actually need to survive, which is my job. I know I mentioned dodging the night runs and taking those crazy detours earlier, but... it's actually worse than that. I’ve already missed two entire shifts this week. I just called in and made up some excuse about being sick, which is terrible because I really need the money, but... man, when I actually do try to work, I genuinely do not feel safe driving. \n\nI'll be sitting there behind the wheel of this big delivery van, and I'm just so shaken and on edge that every time a car honks or a truck backfires, I practically jump out of my seat. It's incredibly dangerous. I'm terrified I'm going to cause a massive accident because my nerves are just entirely shot and I can't focus on the road. And even just the basic stuff, like handling the deliveries, walking up to a receptionist or a homeowner to hand off a package... I'm so jittery and distracted, I can barely look them in the eye or string a normal sentence together. It’s like my whole ability to just function and be around other human beings is completely gone. I just... I don't know how I'm supposed to keep my job or my life together when I can't even hand a guy a cardboard box without feeling like I'm having a heart attack.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened in the holding room?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I don't feel responsible for it. It's not like I asked to be thrown in there, you know? I was just trying to do my job, just dropping off a package like I do a hundred times a week. I didn't do anything wrong, I didn't break any rules, I didn't cause a scene... I was just in the wrong place at the wrong time when they decided to lock down the building. So, no, I don't blame myself for being put in that room. It was completely out of my control. \n\nBut, I mean, even though I know I didn't cause it, it doesn't stop my brain from spinning about it, you know? Like, I'll lie awake at night—when I'm not having those nightmares I told you about—and I'll just retrace every single step of that day. I'll think, \"What if I had taken a different route?\" or \"What if I had stopped for coffee before that delivery?\" or \"What if I had just walked a little faster through the lobby?\" It's like my mind is trying to find a way I could have avoided the whole thing, even though I know logically it wasn't my fault. I was just doing my route. But no, I don't feel guilty. I just... I feel angry, I guess. Angry that it happened to me, angry that I'm dealing with all this now, and angry that I can't just shake it off and go back to normal. But responsible? No. I was just the guy holding the box.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? I was just doing my route. But no, I don't feel guilty. I just... I feel angry, I guess. Angry that it happened to me, angry that I'm dealing with all this now, and angry that I can't just shake it off and go back to normal. But responsible? No. I was just the guy holding the box.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? I... I don't know if I'd say constantly on the lookout, exactly. I mean, I'm definitely jumpy, like I was saying before with the driving, how every little noise makes me flinch. But it's not like I'm walking around scanning rooftops for snipers or checking behind every door before I open it, you know? It's more of a... a reaction thing, rather than me actively searching for danger. \n\nLike, if I'm just sitting in my apartment, I'm not peeking through the blinds or anything. I'm just trying to survive the day. But if the floorboards creak, or if the radiator hisses too loud, I definitely jump. So, I don't think I'm constantly on the lookout, like some kind of bodyguard or something. I'm just really, really sensitive to everything happening around me right now. But actively looking for threats? No, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. I'm too exhausted to be a watchman, honestly. I just want to be left alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startled or frightened by things that wouldn't normally startle you, like a door slamming or a car driving by?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, absolutely. I think I kind of touched on that a second ago with the driving, but it's... it's honestly out of control. It's not just a little flinch, it's like my entire body goes into shock over the smallest things. Like, I was in the van the other day—well, the last day I actually tried to work—and the dispatch radio cracked. Not loud, just the normal static before someone talks. And I swear to you, I practically hit my head on the roof of the cab. I jumped so hard, my hands slipped off the steering wheel. It was like I thought someone was about to rush me right there in the driver's seat. \n\nAnd it's sirens, too. I live in the city, there are sirens all the time, right? Normally, I don't even hear them, they're just background noise. But now? A police car or an ambulance wails three blocks away, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so violently it actually hurts. It just pounds and pounds, and it doesn't stop even after the siren fades. I literally had to pull the van over to the curb twice on Tuesday because my chest was heaving and I couldn't catch my breath just from hearing a fire truck. \n\nEven inside my own building... my neighbor across the hall, he's kind of a heavy-handed guy, always lets his door slam shut when he leaves for work. He's done it for the three years I've lived there. Yesterday morning, he slammed it, and I dropped my coffee mug right on the kitchen floor. It shattered everywhere, and I just stood there shaking, like, physically vibrating, waiting for someone to bust through my door. I had to go step outside onto the fire escape for, like, twenty minutes just to get my heart rate down enough to clean up the mess. It's exhausting. Every sudden noise feels like a physical blow, like an alarm going off in my brain telling me I'm about to be attacked, and I just can't turn the alarm off.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble maintaining relationships with your friends or family due to what's been happening?\n\nPatient: Friends and family? I mean, yeah, but honestly, it’s just... it's everyone. It's not just the people I'm close to, it's literally anyone. I just can't keep it together around people right now, period. Like, I usually do this volunteer thing on the weekends—I've been doing it for a couple of years, just helping out at the community center, setting up chairs and organizing the pantry, that kind of thing—and I just completely stopped showing up for my volunteer commitments. I didn't even call them to explain, because what am I supposed to say? \"Sorry, I can't handle being in a room with other human beings without feeling like I'm going to lose my mind?\" So I just ghosted them, which makes me feel terrible because they rely on me, but I physically can't do it. I can't put on a brave face. \n\nAnd it's bleeding into everything else, too, not just my social life. My whole life is just... it's on pause. Like work. I know I mentioned the van earlier, but it’s gotten to the point where I’ve already missed two whole shifts. I tried to go in, I really did. I put the uniform on and everything. But when I actually try to work, I just... I don't feel safe. I don't feel safe driving that massive van through city traffic, and I definitely don't feel safe walking up to random buildings, going into elevators, and handling deliveries. I'm just too shaken up. I'm so completely on edge that my hands are sweating on the wheel, and I just know I'm going to cause an accident or freak out on a receptionist or something. So yeah, it's affecting my relationships, but it's also just ruining my ability to function at all. I can't work, I can't volunteer, I can't even be around people. I'm just stuck in my apartment.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy things you used to like doing before this incident happened?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? I mean... I don't know if I'd say it's harder to enjoy things, exactly. It's more like... I just haven't even tried? I haven't really been doing anything I usually like to do, so I can't really tell you if I'm not enjoying them. Like, usually on a Thursday night, I'd probably be playing video games, maybe hopping online with a couple of buddies to run a few matches in this shooter game we play. We've been doing that for years, it's kind of our routine. But since this all started, I just... I haven't even turned the console on. \n\nIt's not that I sit there and think, \"Oh, I don't want to play,\" or that I try to play and it's not fun. It's just that the thought of doing it doesn't even cross my mind. Or if it does, it just feels like too much effort. I'm just so drained, you know? Like I said about being stuck in my apartment, I'm just kind of existing in here right now. I'm not putting on music, I'm not watching my usual shows... I tried to watch a movie the other night, just to have some background noise, but I couldn't even tell you what it was about because I was just staring at the wall. So, I guess... I don't know if the enjoyment is gone, or if the energy to even try to enjoy something is gone. I just haven't been doing any of my normal stuff to even find out. I'm just sitting here, trying to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to perform your daily tasks or maintain your usual routine since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I kind of just went off on a tangent about that a minute ago, actually, but... yeah, it's completely destroyed my routine. Everything is just shattered. Like I said, work is... well, work is basically a disaster right now. I'm a courier, right? So my whole job is driving around the city, dealing with traffic, getting in and out of the van, going into office buildings, talking to receptionists, handing off packages. It's a high-energy, fast-paced kind of thing, and you have to be alert. But right now? I can't do it. I've already missed two entire shifts. And the thing is, I really tried to go in. I woke up, I put the uniform on, I drank my coffee, I walked out the door... but the second I get near the van, or even think about being out there on the road, I just freeze up. I don't feel safe. I feel like if I get behind the wheel, I'm going to crash because my hands are shaking so bad, or I'm going to have a panic attack in the middle of an intersection. And handling the deliveries? Walking into a building where I don't know who's in there, or getting into an elevator with strangers? Forget it. I'm just too shaken up, too completely on edge. I feel like a raw nerve.\n\nAnd it's not just work, it's the stuff I actually choose to do, too. My whole weekend routine is gone. I mentioned the volunteer thing... I've been helping out at this community center for a couple of years now. Every Saturday morning, I'd go in, set up chairs for the meetings, organize the food pantry, maybe help unload the delivery truck if it came early. It was a good routine, you know? It got me out of the house, I liked the people there. But I just stopped showing up. Completely ghosted them. And I feel awful about it, I really do, because they count on me to be there to get things set up before the doors open. But I just... I can't keep it together around people right now. The thought of walking into that community center, where it's loud and crowded and there are people everywhere wanting to talk or asking me to move boxes... I just can't handle it. I can't put on a normal face and pretend I'm fine when inside I feel like I'm vibrating out of my skin. So my daily tasks, my routine, my job, my volunteering... it's all just gone. I'm not doing any of it. I'm just stuck here, trying to figure out how to get through the next hour.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like you're able to function normally despite what's been happening?\n\nPatient: Function normally? Honestly, doc, no. Not at all. I mean, I know I literally just went on a whole rant about this a second ago, but... it really is just a complete, across-the-board failure to function right now. There is no \"normal\" area left. \n\nLike I was just saying about work, missing those two shifts wasn't just me needing a mental health day or something. I physically and mentally cannot do it. When I try to work, I just... I don't feel safe driving that van anymore. I don't feel safe navigating the streets or walking up to buildings and handling the deliveries, because I'm just way too shaken. I'm so on edge that the smallest thing makes me want to jump out of my skin, so how am I supposed to do a fast-paced messenger job like that? \n\nAnd it's the exact same reason I completely stopped showing up for my volunteer commitments, which I know I just mentioned too, but it really just highlights how deep this goes. I just can't keep it together around people right now. Not at work, not at the community center, not anywhere. So, no, there isn't a single part of my life that feels intact right now. Everything is just completely derailed. I don't have anywhere I can go or anything I can do where I feel like I'm actually capable of being a functioning person. It's all just broken.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always being on the lookout for potential threats or dangers, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? I... I don't know if I'd say I'm always looking for threats, exactly. It's not like I'm pacing around my apartment peeking through the blinds every five minutes or expecting someone to kick the door down, you know? I mean, I'm definitely jumpy, like I said before. I'm on edge, my nerves are shot, and I feel like I'm vibrating out of my skin half the time. But it's not really this conscious thing where I'm scanning the room for danger or constantly thinking something bad is about to happen right this second. \n\nIt's more like... I just feel unsafe in general, especially if I think about going outside or getting back in the van, but I'm not actively looking for a threat. If anything, I'm just trying to hide from everything. I don't want to see what's out there, I don't want to look for trouble, I just want to sit here and try to keep my heart rate down. So, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout. I'm just overwhelmed by the feeling that everything is too much, rather than looking for a specific reason to be scared, if that makes sense? I'm just trying to avoid the world entirely right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific memories or reminders of the holding room incident that you try to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there definitely are. I mean, it's not even just trying to avoid thinking about it, it's like I'm actively having to rearrange my entire life to avoid anything that even remotely reminds me of it. Like, I told you I'm a courier, right? And usually, I'd just take whatever route is fastest, whatever dispatch gives me, but lately... I just can't. \n\nI've been flat-out turning down any night runs. I used to like them, actually, because the traffic is lighter and you can just cruise, but now? The thought of being out there in the dark, pulling up to some loading dock or side entrance... I can't do it. My chest gets tight just thinking about it. And even during the day, I'm taking the longest, most out-of-the-way routes possible just to make absolutely sure I don't go anywhere near that detention building. If I even see a street sign that points in that direction, I start sweating. I'll add twenty minutes to a drive just to stay on the opposite side of town. \n\nAnd it's not just the place, either. It's talking about it. Like, right now, even just trying to explain this to you, I feel my heart starting to race. If someone asks me why I'm taking a different route, or why I missed my shifts, I just shut down. I can't talk about what actually happened in that holding room. Because the second I start talking about the details, or even just picturing the walls in that place, or the lighting... it's like I'm not in my van anymore, or in my apartment. It feels like I'm right back in that room. The air feels the same, my stomach drops... it's just too real. So I just avoid it all. The building, the night shifts, the conversations... I just have to keep it all as far away from me as possible, or I feel like I'm going to lose my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like you're in a dream when these flashes or memories of the holding room happen?\n\nPatient: Outside of my body? Like... floating above myself or something? No, I... I don't think I've ever felt anything like that. When the flashes happen, when I get those memories of the holding room, it's not like a dream at all. It's actually the exact opposite of a dream. It's too real. It's so real that it feels like it's happening right then and there. \n\nLike I was saying before, about how the air feels the same and my stomach drops? It's not like I'm watching a movie of myself going through it from a distance. I'm *in* it. I'm right back in my own skin, feeling the same panic, the same cold sweat, the same tightness in my chest that I felt when I was actually in that room. It's completely overwhelming, but I'm definitely still inside my own body. If anything, I wish I could feel like I was outside of it, you know? Maybe then it wouldn't be so terrifying. \n\nBut no, it's just... it's a very grounded, very physical feeling of terror. There's no detachment. I don't feel disconnected from myself, I just feel disconnected from where I actually am in the present moment, because my brain is dragging me back to that place. So, no, I haven't had any out-of-body experiences or anything like a dream state. It's just a nightmare that feels entirely real, and I'm wide awake for it."
